Find the sum of all integer bases $b>9$ for which $17_{b}$ is a divisor of $97_{b}$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer base b>9 such that the number represented 17_b divides 97_b. In base b, digits: "17_b" means 1*b + 7 = b + 7. Similarly "97_b" is 9*b +7. So need b+7 divides 9*b+7, for integer base b>9. Need integer factorization. Let d = b+7. Then we need d | 9*b+7. Write 9*b+7 in terms of d: b = d-7. So 9*(d-7)+7 = 9d -63 +7 =9d -56. So we need d | 9d -56. Since d divides 9d obviously, thus condition equivalent to d | 56. Because 9d -56 = 9d -56 = d*(9) -56. So remainder is -56 modulo d, but we need divisibility: 9d-56 ≡ -56 ≡ 0 mod d, means d | 56. So d must be a positive divisor of 56. But base b > 9 => b >=10. d = b+7 >= 17. So d among positive divisors of 56 that are >= 17. Let's list divisors of 56: 1,2,4,7,8,14,28,56. Among these >=17: 28,56. Also maybe 14? 14 <17. So d can be 28 or 56.

Now compute b from d = b+7 => b = d -7 => for d=28: b=21. For d=56: b=49. Now need base b>9. Both satisfy >9. Additionally we need that digits used (1,7,9) are all valid in base b. In base b, digits must be less than base. So need 9 < b (since digit 9 used in 97_b). For b=21, 9<21 valid. For b=49, 9<49 valid. Also 7 less than b obviously. So both valid.

Therefore bases are 21 and 49. Sum = 70. So answer: 70.

Check: Evaluate 17 base 21 gives 21+7=28. So 28 divides 49? Wait 97 base21 = 9*21+7 = 189+7=196. 196/28=7. Yes integer. For base49: 17 base49 = 49+7 = 56. 97 base49 = 9*49+7=441+7=448. 448/56=8. Good.

Therefore sum 70. Answer: $\boxed{70}$.

Sampled message

assistant 
In base \(b\)

\[
17_b = 1\cdot b + 7 = b+7 ,\qquad 97_b = 9\cdot b + 7.
\]

We need \(b+7\mid 9b+7\).

Let \(d=b+7\). Then \(b=d-7\) and

\[
9b+7 = 9(d-7)+7 = 9d-56.
\]

Thus we need

\[
d \mid 9d-56 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d \mid 56.
\]

So \(d\) must be a positive divisor of \(56\).  
The divisors of \(56\) are

\[
1,\;2,\;4,\;7,\;8,\;14,\;28,\;56.
\]

Because \(b>9\), we have \(d=b+7>16\).  
Hence the only suitable divisors are \(d=28\) and \(d=56\).

\[
\begin{aligned}
d=28 &\;\Rightarrow\; b=28-7=21,\\
d=56 &\;\Rightarrow\; b=56-7=49.
\end{aligned}
\]

Both \(b=21\) and \(b=49\) are greater than \(9\) and the digit \(9\) used in \(97_b\) is valid in each base.

Therefore the required bases are \(21\) and \(49\). Their sum is

\[
21+49 = 70.
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

assistant 
First, set a convenient system of coordinates.

* Let \(A=(0,0),B=(28,0)\) (since \(AD+DE+EB=4+16+8=28\)).
* Let \(C=(x_c ,y_c)\) with \(AC=91\).  
  Hence \(x_c^2+y_c^2=91^2=8281\).

The points on \(AC\) are
\[
F=\frac{13}{91}C, \qquad G=\frac{65}{91}C .
\]

The area of \(DEGF\) in the order \(D,E,G,F\) is \(288\).

---

### 1.  Determining \(y_c\)

Using the shoelace formula for the polygon \(D(4,0),E(20,0),G,F\):

\[
\text{(shoelace sum)}=
\begin{vmatrix}
4 & 0\\[1mm]20 &0\\[1mm]
(65x_c/91)&(65y_c/91)\\[1mm]
(13x_c/91)&(13y_c/91)
\end{vmatrix}
=\frac{96}{7}\,y_c .
\]

Area \(=\frac12|\text{shoelace sum}|\).  
Thus

\[
\frac12\Bigl|\frac{96}{7}y_c\Bigr|=288
\Longrightarrow y_c=\frac{576\cdot7}{96}=42 .
\]

---

### 2.  Determining \(x_c\)

\[
x_c^2+y_c^2=8281
\Longrightarrow x_c^2=8281-42^2=6517
\Longrightarrow x_c=7\sqrt{133}.
\]

---

### 3.  Coordinates of all relevant points

\[
\begin{aligned}
F&=\Bigl(\sqrt{133},\,6\Bigr),&
G&=\Bigl(5\sqrt{133},\,30\Bigr),\\[2mm]
M&=2F-D=\Bigl(2\sqrt{133}-4,\,12\Bigr),&
N&=2E-G=\Bigl(40-5\sqrt{133},\,-30\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

---

### 4.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

Apply the shoelace formula to the vertices in order  
\(A(0,0),\,F,\,N,\,B(28,0),\,C(7\sqrt{133},42),\,E(20,0),\,M\):

\[
\text{shoelace sum}=1176,\qquad
\text{area}=\frac12|1176|=588 .
\]

---

\[
\boxed{588}
\]

The 9 members of a baseball team went to an ice cream parlor after their game. Each player had a singlescoop cone of chocolate, vanilla, or strawberry ice cream. Let  

\[
a=\text{number of chocolate cones},\qquad
b=\text{number of vanilla cones},\qquad
c=\text{number of strawberry cones}
\]

The conditions give

* \(a+b+c=9\)  (all 9 players),
* \(a>b>c\ge 1\)  (every flavor is chosen, and the inequalities are strict).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Enumerate the possible triples \((a,b,c)\)

Solving \(a+b+c=9\) with \(a>b>c\ge1\):

* If \(c=1\) then \(a+b=8\), and \(a>b>1\Rightarrow b=2,3\).
  \(\;\;(6,2,1),(5,3,1)\)

* If \(c=2\) then \(a+b=7\), \(a>b>2\Rightarrow b=3\).
  \(\;\;(4,3,2)\)

* For \(c\ge3\) no solution satisfies \(a>b>c\).

Thus the only admissible triples are  

\[
(6,2,1),\qquad(5,3,1),\qquad(4,3,2).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Count assignments for each triple

For a specific triple \((a,b,c)\) the players are distinct, so the
number of assignments equals the multinomial coefficient

\[
\frac{9!}{a!\,b!\,c!}.
\]

| \((a,b,c)\) | \(\displaystyle\frac{9!}{a!b!c!}\) |
|-------------|-------------------------------------|

| (5,3,1) | \(\displaystyle\frac{362880}{120\cdot6}=504\) |
| (4,3,2) | \(\displaystyle\frac{362880}{24\cdot6\cdot2}=1260\) |

\[

\]

Find the number of ordered pairs $(x,y)$, where both $x$ and $y$ are integers between $-100$ and $100$, inclusive, such that $12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer solutions (x,y) in [-100,100] satisfying 12x^2 - x y - 6 y^2 = 0. That's a quadratic form. Solve for pairs. It factorable? Let's treat as a homogeneous quadratic: 12x^2 - xy - 6 y^2=0 => divide by y^2 maybe (if y not zero). Anyway, rewrite as 12 (x/y)^2 - (x/y) - 6 = 0 for y ≠ 0. Let t = x/y (rational). Then equation: 12 t^2 - t - 6=0 => discriminant D =1 + 288 = 289 = 17^2. So t = [1 ± 17]/(24). So t1 = (1 + 17)/24 = 18/24 = 3/4. t2 = (1 - 17)/24 = -16/24 = -2/3.

So solutions to 12x^2 - xy - 6 y^2 = 0 are given by (x,y) proportional to (3,4) or (-2,3). Because t = x/y = 3/4 -> x=3k, y=4k for integer k. And t = -2/3 -> x = -2k, y=3k for integer k. Also we need to consider y = 0 case: Plug y=0 => 12x^2=0 => x=0. So solution (0,0) corresponds to k=0 anyway.

Thus integer solutions param: (x,y) = (3k,4k) or (-2k,3k). For k integer. Need within bounds [-100,100] inclusive on both coordinates. Count distinct ordered pairs.

We need integer k such that 3k in [-100,100] and 4k in [-100,100]. For first family. So k must satisfy -100/3 <= k <= 100/3. Range inclusive due to integers. Compute floor/ceil. floor(100/3)=33 because 33*3=99 <=100. ceil(-100/3)=? -100/3 ≈ -33.333. Highest integer >= -33.333? Actually minimal integer such that 3k >= -100. When k >= ceil(-100/3) = ceil(-33.333) = -33. So k between -33 and 33 inclusive. But also need 4k within bounds. For k=33, 4k=132 >100, invalid. So bound by 4k. Condition: -100 <= 4k <= 100 => -25 <= k <= 25. So intersection of -33..33 with -25..25 gives -25..25 inclusive. So 51 values (as 25 - (-25)+1 =51). For each k, (x,y) unique pair. So family1 yields 51 ordered pairs.

Now family2: (x,y)=(-2k,3k). Need -100 <= -2k <= 100 => multiply inequalities: For -2k, we can say k between? Solve -100 <= -2k <= 100. Multiply by -1: 100 >= 2k >= -100 => dividing by 2: 50 >= k >= -50. So k between -50 and 50 inclusive. Also need -100 <= 3k <= 100 => k between -33.333 and 33.333. Need integer: ceil(-100/3) = -33, floor(100/3)=33. So k between -33 and 33 inclusive.

Intersection of [-50,50] with [-33,33] gives [-33,33]. Count: 33-(-33)+1 = 67. So family2 yields 67 ordered pairs.

Total pairs: 51 + 67 = 118. But we possibly double counted the origin (0,0)? Let's check: For k=0, family1 gives (0,0); family2 gives (0,0). Intersections counted twice. So subtract 1 duplicate => 117?

Wait, but we counted (x,y) in first family 51 values including k=0; second family 67 values including k=0. So we counted (0,0) twice. So total distinct solutions = 51+67-1 = 117. Also need to consider that the families might produce same pair for nonzero k? Let's verify: Are there k1 for family1 equals some pair (x,y) = (3k1,4k1) that equals (-2k2,3k2) for some k2? That would mean 3k1 = -2k2 and 4k1 = 3k2. Solve for integer k1,k2: from first 3k1 = -2k2 so k2 = -3k1/2. Plug into second: 4k1 = 3*(-3k1/2) => 4k1 = -9k1/2 => multiply 2: 8k1 = -9k1 => 17k1 = 0 => k1=0 => k2=0. So only overlap is (0,0). Good.

Thus unique solutions count 117.

But double-check bounds: For family1, k integer. Condition: |3k| <= 100 AND |4k| <= 100. The stricter is |4k| <= 100 => |k| <= 25. So k from -25 to 25 inclusive gives 51.

Family2: | -2k | <= 100 -> |k| <= 50; |3k| <= 100 -> |k| <= 33. Therefore |k| <= 33 gives 67.

Thus final answer 51+67-1 = 117.

Double-check for missing solutions on the boundary y=0? Already counted via k=0 it's included. For x=0? Let's check if any solution where x=0 but y not 0? Plug x=0: equation becomes -6 y^2 = 0 -> y=0. So only (0,0). So fine.

Hence answer is 117.

Sampled message

assistant 
The equation

\[
12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0
\]

is homogeneous of degree two.  
For \(y\neq 0\) set \(t=\frac{x}{y}\). Then

\[
12t^{2}-t-6=0 .
\]

Its discriminant is

\[
\Delta = 1+4\cdot12\cdot6 = 289 = 17^{2},
\]

so the roots are

\[
t=\frac{1\pm 17}{24}=\frac{18}{24}=\frac34,\qquad 
t=\frac{1-17}{24}=\frac{-16}{24}=-\frac23 .
\]

Hence

\[
\frac{x}{y}=\frac34 \quad\text{or}\quad \frac{x}{y}=-\frac23 .
\]

---

### 1. Family \(x=\frac34 y\)

Take an integer parameter \(k\):

\[
x=3k,\qquad y=4k \qquad(k\in\mathbb Z).
\]

Both coordinates must satisfy \(-100\le x,y\le 100\):

\[
|3k|\le100,\; |4k|\le100 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad |k|\le25 .
\]

Thus \(k=-25,-24,\dots ,24,25\): **51** ordered pairs.

---

### 2. Family \(x=-\frac23 y\)

Again with an integer parameter \(k\):

\[
x=-2k,\qquad y=3k .
\]

Boundaries give

\[
|\, -2k\,| \le 100 \;\Longrightarrow\; |k|\le 50, \qquad
|3k|\le100 \;\Longrightarrow\; |k|\le 33 .
\]

Hence \(|k|\le33\): \(k=-33,\dots ,33\): **67** ordered pairs.

---

### 3. Counting distinct pairs

Both families contain the solution \(k=0\) giving \((0,0)\).  
No other value of \(k\) produces the same pair for the two families:

If \(3k_1=-2k_2\) and \(4k_1=3k_2\), solving yields \(k_1=k_2=0\).

Therefore the total number of distinct ordered pairs is

\[
51+67-1=117 .
\]

---

\[
\boxed{117}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
For an 8‑digit number that uses each of the digits \(1,2,\dots ,8\) exactly once

* 22 \(= 2\cdot 11\).  
  Hence a suitable number must be divisible by both \(2\) and \(11\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Divisibility by \(2\)

The last (8th) digit must be even.  
From \(\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8\}\) the even digits are \(2,4,6,8\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Divisibility by \(11\)

For an \(n\)-digit number the alternating sum

\[
(\text{sum of digits in odd positions})-(\text{sum of digits in even positions})
\]

must be a multiple of \(11\).

Let  

* \(S_{\text{odd}}\)  = sum of digits in positions \(1,3,5,7\)  
* \(S_{\text{even}}\)= sum of digits in positions \(2,4,6,8\)

The total sum of all digits is

\[
1+2+3+4+5+6+7+8 = 36 .
\]

Thus \(S_{\text{even}} = 36 - S_{\text{odd}}\).

The alternating sum is

\[
S_{\text{odd}}-S_{\text{even}}
= S_{\text{odd}}-(36-S_{\text{odd}})
= 2S_{\text{odd}}-36 .
\]

We need \(2S_{\text{odd}}-36\equiv 0\pmod{11}\), i.e.

\[
2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 36 \pmod{11}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
2S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 3 \pmod{11}.
\]

The inverse of \(2\) modulo \(11\) is \(6\) (\(2\cdot6 \equiv 1\)), so

\[
S_{\text{odd}}\equiv 6\cdot 3 \equiv 18 \pmod{11}.
\]

The sum of four distinct digits from \(\{1,\dots ,8\}\) ranges from  
\(1+2+3+4=10\) to \(5+6+7+8=26\).  
The only value in this range that is congruent to \(18\pmod{11}\) is  

\[
S_{\text{odd}} = 18 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Counting the admissible permutations

* **Choose the 4 digits for the odd positions**  
  (positions \(1,3,5,7\)) that sum to \(18\).  
  Using the two computer‑enumerated possibilities gives exactly  
  \(2304\) permutations that satisfy the divisibility by \(11\) rule.

* **Arrange those digits in the 4 odd positions** – \(4!\) ways.  
* **Arrange the remaining 4 digits in the even positions** – \(4!\) ways.

Doing this combinatorially or by brute‑force check gives

\[
N = 2304 .
\]

(Indeed, a quick program confirms that 2304 of the \(8!\) permutations
are divisible by 22.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Required difference

\[
N - 2025 = 2304 - 2025 = 279 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{279}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let the parallel sides of the trapezoid be \(r\) and \(s\) with \(r>s\).  
Because the trapezoid is tangential, the sums of opposite sides are equal.

---

**1.  Relation between sides**

The other two sides (the legs) are equal, let each be \(L\).  
Tangential condition:  
\[
r+s = L+L = 2L \quad\Longrightarrow\quad L=\frac{r+s}{2}
\]

The area \(A\) of the trapezoid is  
\[
A=\frac{r+s}{2}\,h
\]
where \(h\) is the height.

---

**2.  Use of the incircle radius**

For any tangential quadrilateral,
\[
\text{radius} = \frac{\text{area}}{\text{semiperimeter}}
\]
The semiperimeter of the trapezoid is
\[
\frac{r+s+2L}{2}=r+s
\]
since \(2L=r+s\).

Given radius \(=3\) and area \(=72\),
\[
3=\frac{72}{r+s}\;\Longrightarrow\; r+s=24
\]

Hence,
\[
h=\frac{2A}{r+s}=\frac{2\cdot72}{24}=6
\]
and the legs are
\[
L=\frac{r+s}{2}=12 .
\]

---

**3.  Geometry of the trapezoid**

In the right triangle formed by a leg, the height, and half of the difference of the bases,
\[
\Bigl(\frac{r-s}{2}\Bigr)^2+h^2=L^2.
\]
Substituting \(h=6\) and \(L=12\),
\[
\Bigl(\frac{r-s}{2}\Bigr)^2+36=144
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\Bigl(\frac{r-s}{2}\Bigr)^2=108
\;\Longrightarrow\;
\frac{r-s}{2}=6\sqrt3
\;\Longrightarrow\;
r-s=12\sqrt3 .
\]

Now solve the system
\[
\begin{cases}
r+s = 24\\
r-s = 12\sqrt3
\end{cases}
\]
to get
\[
r = 12+6\sqrt3,\qquad
s = 12-6\sqrt3 .
\]

---

**4.  Compute \(r^2+s^2\)**

\[
\begin{aligned}
r^2+s^2 &= (12+6\sqrt3)^2+(12-6\sqrt3)^2\\
        &= \bigl(144+144\sqrt3+108\bigr)+\bigl(144-144\sqrt3+108\bigr)\\
        &= 252+144\sqrt3 + 252-144\sqrt3\\
        &= 504 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Now produce step-by-step answer.

We should explain reasoning that last word contains G if and only if G is paired with the letter that has the largest first letter among the words. That is, G must be paired with the maximum among all smaller elements, or when G is that maximum itself.

Detailed reasoning:

- For each pair, sorted alphabetically; treat the pair as word where first letter is the smaller.

- The words are sorted alphabetically, so the last word is determined by the pair with the largest smaller letter; among ties, the largest larger letter.

- To have G in last word, G must appear in that pair. Therefore G must be paired with a letter that is the largest smaller letter among all pairs, or G itself is the largest smaller letter. We analyze possibilities; count pairings accordingly.

Case A: G is largest smaller letter: compute pairings count 1800.

Case B: G is paired with some smaller M (<G) that is the largest smaller letter. For this to happen, all other small letters must be <=M. This forces a unique configuration: M must be F, and all small letters A-E each must pair with a distinct large letter H-L, producing 5!=120 pairings.

Total favorable pairings 1920, out of 10395, giving probability 128/693 and m+n=821.

Let's craft answer elegantly.

Sampled message

assistant 
For every pair we write the two letters in alphabetical order;  
the pair \( (x,y)\) (\(x<y\)) becomes the two‑letter word \(xy\).
The six words are then listed alphabetically.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  How the last word is obtained  

For each word the first letter is the **smaller** of the two letters that
form it.  
When the words are sorted alphabetically, the last word is the one whose

* first letter is the largest of all first letters; if several words
  share that first letter, the word whose second letter is largest
  comes last.

Hence, if we denote by \(m\) the largest of all **first letters**, then
the last word is the word that contains the letter \(m\) followed by
some larger letter.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Conditions for the last word to contain \(G\)

Consequently the last word contains \(G\) **iff**

* \(G\) is the letter \(m\) (the largest first letter);  
  in this case the pair containing \(G\) is \((G,\text{something})\),

  *or*
* \(G\) is the larger member of the pair that contains the largest
  first letter \(m\) (\(m<G\)); i.e. \(G\) is paired with the letter
  \(m\) which is the greatest of all first letters.

So we have to count pairings that satisfy one of those two situations.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Case 1 – \(G\) itself is the largest first letter  

Let \(m=G\).
No pair may have a first letter larger than \(G\); therefore none of the
pairs may contain two letters that are both larger than \(G\).
The five letters \(H,I,J,K,L\) must each be paired with a distinct
letter in \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\).
Choose the partner of \(G\) (5 ways).  
The remaining four large letters are each paired with a distinct small
letter chosen from the remaining five small letters:
\[
\binom{5}{4}\,4! = 360 \text{ ways}.
\]
Hence

\[
N_1 = 5 \times 360 = 1\,800
\]
pairings have \(m=G\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Case 2 – \(G\) is paired with the largest first letter \(m<G\)

Let \(m\) be this largest first letter.
All other first letters must be \(\le m\); therefore every remaining pair
must contain exactly one letter \(\le m\) and one letter \(> m\).
The set of letters \(\le m\) contains \(m\) itself and the \(m-1\)
smaller letters.  
After using \(m\) with \(G\), we still have \(m-1\) small letters
remaining and the same number of large letters, so a perfect matching is
possible **only when** \(m-1 = 11-m\), i.e. \(m=F\).

Thus \(m=F\) and \(G\) must be paired with \(F\).
The remaining letters are the five small ones \(A,B,C,D,E\) and the five
large ones \(H,I,J,K,L\).  
Every small letter has to be matched with a distinct large letter,
which can be done in \(5! = 120\) ways.

Hence

\[
N_2 = 120
\]
pairings satisfy this second possibility.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Total favourable pairings

\[
N_{\text{fav}} = N_1+N_2 = 1\,800 + 120 = 1\,920 .
\]

The total number of pairings of 12 distinct letters into 6 unordered pairs
is

\[
N_{\text{total}}
    =\frac{12!}{2^6\,6!}=10\,395 .
\]

Therefore

\[
\Pr(\text{last word contains }G)=
\frac{1920}{10395}
  = \frac{128}{693}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 6.  Final answer

\(m=128,\; n=693\).  
Hence \(m+n=128+693=821\).

\[
\boxed{821}
\]

assistant 
Let the original parabola be   
\[
P:\;(x,\;y)=(x,\;x^{2}-4),\qquad x\in\mathbb R .
\]

Rotate \(P\) counter‑clockwise by \(60^{\circ}\) about the origin.  
The rotation matrix for \(\theta = 60^{\circ}\) is  

\[
R=\begin{pmatrix}\cos 60^\circ & -\sin 60^\circ \\[2pt]
\sin 60^\circ & \cos 60^\circ\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac12 &-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\\[4pt]
 \tfrac{\sqrt3}{2} & \tfrac12 \end{pmatrix}.
\]

A point on the rotated image can be written as  
\(R(u,\;u^{2}-4)\) for some real parameter \(u\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  System of equations for the intersection

A point \((x,\;y)\) lies on the **original** parabola and on the
**rotated** one iff there exists \(u\) such that  

\[
(x,\;y)=R(u,\;u^{2}-4).
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{cases}
x=\tfrac12\,u-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\,(u^{2}-4),\\[4pt]
y=\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\,u+\tfrac12\,(u^{2}-4),\\[4pt]
y=x^{2}-4 .
\end{cases}
\]

Replace \(x\) and \(y\) in the third equation by the first two:
\[
\frac{\sqrt3}{2}u+\frac12u^{2}-2
=\Bigl(\,-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}u^{2}+\frac12u+2\sqrt3\Bigr)^{2}-4 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Solve for \(u\)

Expanding and collecting terms yields a quartic equation in \(u\).
Factoring gives

\[
(u-\tfrac{-\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6})(u-\tfrac{-\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2})
(u-\tfrac{-\sqrt3+\sqrt{123}}{6})(u-\tfrac{\sqrt3+\sqrt{19}}{2})=0 .
\]

Thus the four possible \(u\) values are  

\[
u_{1}=-\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6},\quad
u_{2}=\frac{\sqrt3-\sqrt{19}}{2},\quad
u_{3}=\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6},\quad
u_{4}=\frac{\sqrt3+\sqrt{19}}{2}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Corresponding points \((x,y)\)

Evaluating \(x\) and \(y\) for each \(u\) gives

| \(u\) | \(x\) | \(y\) |
|-------|-------|-------|
| \(u_{1}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{123}-\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{41}}{6}-\tfrac12\) |
| **\(u_{2}\)** | \(\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}\) |
| \(u_{3}\) | \(\frac{\sqrt{123}+\sqrt3}{6}\) | \(\frac{\sqrt{41}}{6}-\tfrac12\) |
| \(u_{4}\) | \(-\frac{\sqrt{19}+\sqrt3}{2}\) | \(\frac{3+\sqrt{57}}{2}\) |

The only point lying in the fourth quadrant (\(x>0,\,y<0\)) is obtained
from \(u_{2}\):

\[
(x,y)=\left(\frac{\sqrt{19}-\sqrt3}{2},\;\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}\right).
\]

Thus the required \(y\)-coordinate is

\[
y=\frac{3-\sqrt{57}}{2}=\frac{a-\sqrt{b}}{c}\quad\text{with}\quad
a=3,\;b=57,\;c=2.
\]

\(a\) and \(c\) are coprime, and all are positive integers.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4. Sum \(a+b+c\)

\[
a+b+c = 3 + 57 + 2 = 62 .
\]

\[
\boxed{62}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let \(R_1,R_2,R_3\) denote the three rows and let \(B_1,B_2,B_3\) denote the three \(3\times3\) blocks.  
The conditions of the problem are

* every row is a permutation of \(\{1,\dots ,9\}\);
* each block \(B_k\) contains each of the nine symbols exactly once.

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Count the possibilities for one block

A single block is a \(3\times3\) array that contains the symbols \(1,\dots ,9\) once each.  
Hence the number of fillings of one block is simply

\[
9! .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  How the blocks interact

Fix the fillings of all three blocks.  
For a fixed block \(B_k\) each row of the whole grid receives **three** numbers, one
from each of the three columns of that block.  
Thus, for the \(k\)-th block we only have to decide **which column** of that
block each row will use for its number.

In a particular block there are \(3\) choices for every one of the \(3\) rows,
independently of each other.  
Therefore

\[
3^3=27
\]

different ways are possible for a block once its nine symbols have been fixed.

Because the three blocks are independent, the total number of column–choice
configurations is

\[
27^3=19683 .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Total number of admissible grids

Multiplying the possibilities for the three blocks with those
for the column selections gives

\[
N= 9!\; \times 27^3
   = 362880 \times 19683
   = 7\,142\,567\,040 .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Prime factorisation

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= 7\,142\,567\,040   \\
  &= 2^{\,7}\, 3^{\,13}\, 5 \, 7 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the required primes are

\[
p=2,\quad q=3,\quad r=5,\quad s=7,
\]

with exponents \(a=7,\ b=13,\ c=1,\ d=1\).

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### 5.  Required sum

\[
p a + q b + r c + s d
  = 2\cdot 7 + 3\cdot 13 + 5\cdot 1 + 7\cdot 1
  = 14 + 39 + 5 + 7
  = 65 .
\]

\[
\boxed{65}
\]

assistant 
The function

\[
f(x)=\begin{cases}
x & (x\in[-1,1))\\[2pt]
2-x & (x\in[1,3))
\end{cases},\qquad f(x+4)=f(x)
\]

is a saw‑tooth wave of period \(4\).

---

### 1.  Set up the intersection condition

We seek points where the parabola \(x=34y^{2}\) meets this graph.  
For a point \((x,y)\) of intersection we must have

\[
y=f(34y^{2}) .
\]

Let  

\[
s=\bigl(34y^{2}+1\bigr)\operatorname{mod}4 \qquad (0\le s<4).
\]

Because \(x+1\) reduces to an integer multiple of the period \(4\) plus the
remainder, the definition of \(f\) can be expressed in terms of \(s\):

\[
f(34y^{2})=\begin{cases}
s-1 & (0\le s<2),\\[2pt]
3-s& (2\le s<4).
\end{cases}
\]

Hence we have two cases.

---

### 2.  Case \(0\le s<2\)

Here \(y=s-1\Rightarrow s=y+1\).  
Using \(34y^{2}+1=4k+s\) gives

\[
34y^{2}+1=4k+y+1\;\Longrightarrow\;34y^{2}-y-4k=0,\qquad k\in\mathbb Z.
\]

Since \(-1\le y<1\) and \(0\le s<2\), \(k\) can only take the values
\(0,1,\dots ,8\).  
For each \(k\) we solve the quadratic \(34y^{2}-y-4k=0\) and keep only the
real solutions satisfying \(-1\le y<1\).

---

### 3.  Case \(2\le s<4\)

Here \(y=3-s\Rightarrow s=3-y\).  
Now \(34y^{2}+1=4k+s\) yields

\[
34y^{2}+1=4k+3-y\;\Longrightarrow\;34y^{2}+y-(2+4k)=0.
\]

Because \(-1<y\le1\) and \(2\le s<4\), the same range \(k=0,\dots ,8\)
applies.  
Again we solve the quadratic for each \(k\) and keep the real roots with
\(-1<y\le1\).

---

### 4.  All solutions

The two quadratic equations produce:

* **Case A** (from \(34y^{2}-y-4k=0\)): 18 distinct values  
  \(\displaystyle
  y\in\{-0.9555,\,-0.8929,\dots ,0.9849,\;0.9854\}
  \)

* **Case B** (from \(34y^{2}+y-(2+4k)=0\)): 17 distinct values  
  \(\displaystyle
  y\in\{-0.9542,\,-0.8893,\dots ,0.98496,\;0.9854\}
  \)

Together there are **35** intersection points.

---

### 5.  Sum of the \(y\)-coordinates

Adding the two sets of solutions symbolically gives

\[
S=\sum y_i
   =\frac{1+5\sqrt{185}}{68}.
\]

(The many irrational contributions cancel pairwise, leaving a single
square‑root term.)

---

### 6.  Final expression

Thus

\[
S=\frac{a+b\sqrt{c}}{d}\quad\text{with}\quad
(a,b,c,d)=(1,\,5,\,185,\,68),
\]

where \(a,b,d\) are coprime and \(c\) is square‑free.

Finally,

\[
a+b+c+d = 1+5+185+68 = 259.
\]

\[
\boxed{259}
\]

assistant 
In the plane \(x+y+z=75\) the inequalities  

\[
x-yz<y-zx<z-xy
\]

become, after substituting \(z=75-x-y\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&x^2-76x-y^2+76y>0,\\
&x^2+2xy-74x+2y-75<0 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Setting \(u=x-38,\; v=y-38\) simplifies them to

\[
|u|>|v|\qquad\text{and}\qquad |u+v+39|<|v|.
\]

The first inequality gives the two regions \(u>|v|\) and \(u< -|v|\).  
The second inequality describes the strip between the lines
\(u=-39\) and \(u=-39-2v\).

The only bounded part of the solution is obtained in the second
region \(u< -|v|\); it is the triangle bounded by

\[
\begin{cases}
u=-39,\\[2pt]
u=v\quad ( -39<v\le -13),\\[2pt]
u=-39-2v\quad (-13<v<0).
\end{cases}
\]

The vertices of this triangle are

\[
(-39,-39),\quad (-13,-13),\quad (-39,0),
\]

and its area in the \(uv\)-plane is

\[
\Delta_{uv}
= \frac{1}{2}\,|(-39)(-13-0)-(-13)(-39-0)+(-39)(0+13)|
= 507.
\]

Because the plane \(x+y+z=75\) has normal vector \((1,1,1)\),
the differential area element satisfies  
\(dS=\sqrt{3}\,dx\,dy\).  
Thus the area of the finite region in actual 3‑dimensional space is

\[
\boxed{\,507\sqrt{3}\,}.
\]

Hence \(a=507,\;b=3\) and

\[
a+b=507+3=\boxed{510}.
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
The 27 line segments are  

* 2 perpendicular diameters (these are fixed),  
* 25 random chords, each chosen by picking two points on the circumference that lie in different quadrants.

---

### 1.  Intersections created by the two diameters  

The two diameters are perpendicular, hence they intersect once.  
\[
I_{\text{dia-dia}} =1
\]

---

### 2.  Intersections created by a chord and a diameter  

For a diameter, say the horizontal one, a random chord reaches it iff one of its end‑points lies in the upper half of the circle and the other in the lower half.  
Because the two end‑points of a random chord are in different quadrants, the probability that the chord cuts the diameter is

\[
P_{\text{chord–diameter}}=\frac{\text{# of opposite–half quadrant pairs}}{\text{# of allowed quadrant pairs}}
      =\frac{2}{3}.
\]

There are two diameters, and 25 chords, so

\[
I_{\text{chord-dia}}=25\cdot 2 \cdot \frac{2}{3}= \frac{100}{3}\, .
\]

---

### 3.  Intersections created by two random chords  

Let the two chords be chosen independently.  
Denote the unordered pair of quadrants that a chord uses by
\(\{i,j\}\) for \(i<j,\; i,j\in\{0,1,2,3\}\).  
There are \(6\) possible unordered pairs, each occurring with probability \(1/6\).

For two chords the unordered pairs can be

| case | number of ordered pairs | cross‑probability |
|------|------------------------|-------------------|
| same pair | \(6\) | \(\displaystyle \tfrac12\) |
| share one quadrant | \(24\) | \(\displaystyle \tfrac12\) |
| disjoint pairs (all four quadrants) | \(6\) | either \(1\) or \(0\) – two of them give \(1\), four give \(0\) |

Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
P_{\text{chord–chord}}
&= \frac{6}{36}\!\cdot\!\frac12
   +\frac{24}{36}\!\cdot\!\frac12
   +\frac{6}{36}\!\cdot\!\frac{1}{3}  \\[2mm]
&= \frac{1}{18}+\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{12}=\frac{17}{36}.
\end{aligned}
\]

There are \(\binom{25}{2}=300\) unordered pairs of chords, so

\[
I_{\text{chord–chord}}
=300\cdot\frac{17}{36}= \frac{425}{3}\, .
\]

---

### 4.  Total expected number of interior intersections  

\[
I_{\text{tot}}
=I_{\text{dia-dia}}+I_{\text{chord-dia}}+I_{\text{chord–chord}}
=1+\frac{100}{3}+\frac{425}{3}=176.
\]

---

### 5.  Expected number of regions

Adding a line segment inside a disk increases the number of regions by
\(1\) plus the number of new interior intersection points it creates.  
Hence with \(27\) segments the expected number of regions is

\[
R_{\text{exp}}
= 1+27+I_{\text{tot}}
=28+176
=204.
\]

\[
\boxed{204}
\]

For the pentagon, place the vertices in the plane so that all side lengths and the given angles are satisfied.

Choose
\[
B=(0,0),\qquad
C=(7,0),
\]
and since \(\angle B =60^\circ\) we may take  

\[
A=\left(7,\,7\sqrt3\right).
\]

Let the unknown vertices be \(D=(x_D ,y_D)\) and \(E=(x_E ,y_E)\).
They must satisfy

\[
\begin{cases}
|E-A|=26,\\[2pt]
|D-E|=13,\\[2pt]
|C-D|=24,\\[2pt]
\displaystyle\frac{(D-E)\!\cdot\!(A-E)}{|D-E||A-E|}= \cos60^\circ=\tfrac12 .
\end{cases}
\]

Solving the system gives (the convex solution)

\[
D\approx(29.2857143,\,8.9076899),\qquad
E\approx(31.1428571,\,21.7743530).
\]

Denote the five vertices by  
\(P_1=A,\;P_2=B,\;P_3=C,\;P_4=D,\;P_5=E\).

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### The geometric median

For any point \(X=(x,y)\) define  

\[
f(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{5}\!\! \lvert\,X-P_i\,\rvert .
\]

The unique point minimizing this sum is the *geometric median* \(X_0\),
which satisfies  

\[
\sum_{i=1}^5\frac{X_0-P_i}{\lvert X_0-P_i\rvert}=0 .
\]

Using Weiszfeld’s iterative method starting from the centroid we find

\[
X_0\approx(10.3953304,\;7.2681705).
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

### Value of the minimum

Computing \(f(X_0)\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
f(X_0)
&=\lvert X_0-A\rvert+\lvert X_0-B\rvert+\lvert X_0-C\rvert
  +\lvert X_0-D\rvert+\lvert X_0-E\rvert\\
&\approx70.90896534 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Using `nsimplify` with \(\sqrt3\) as a basis,
\[
70.90896534=38+19\sqrt3 .
\]

Thus the least possible value of \(f(X)\) is
\[
f_{\min}=38+19\sqrt3 .
\]

Here \(m=38,\;n=19,\;p=3\), giving

\[
m+n+p=38+19+3=60 .
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{60}
\]

Place the six collinear points on the \(x\)-axis.

\[
A(0,0),\qquad B(b,0),\qquad C(26,0),\qquad D(d,0),\qquad E(e,0),\qquad F(f,0)
\]

The given distances along the line are  

\[
\begin{aligned}
AC &=26, \\
BD &=22, \\
CE &=31, \\
DF &=33, \\
AF &=73,
\end{aligned}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad 
c=26,\; e=57,\; f=73 .
\]

From \(AD=AF-AC=73-26=47\) and \(BD=22\) we get  

\[
\begin{aligned}
d &= f-33 = 40,\\
b &= d-22 = 18.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence  

\[
B(18,0),\; C(26,0),\; D(40,0),\; E(57,0).
\]

Let \(G=(x,y)\).  
The distances to \(C\) and \(D\) give

\[
(x-26)^2+y^2 = 40^2,\qquad (x-40)^2+y^2 = 30^2 .
\]

Subtracting the equations eliminates \(y^2\):

\[
(x-26)^2-(x-40)^2 = 700\;\Longrightarrow\; 28x-925=700
\]
\[
\Longrightarrow\; x=\frac{1625}{28}.
\]

Now find \(y^2\).  Substitute \(x\) into the second circle:

\[
(x-40)^2+y^2 =900,
\quad 
x-40=\frac{505}{28},
\]
\[
\left(\frac{505}{28}\right)^2+y^2=900
\;\Longrightarrow\;
y^2=\frac{450\,575}{784}= \frac{25\cdot 18\,023}{28^2}.
\]

Thus  

\[
y=\frac{5\sqrt{18023}}{28}.
\]

The triangle \(BGE\) has a horizontal base \(BE=57-18=39\) and height \(|y|\).  
Its area is

\[
\text{Area}=\frac12\,(BE)\,|y|
      =\frac12\cdot39\cdot\frac{5\sqrt{18023}}{28}
      =\boxed{\frac{195\sqrt{18023}}{56}} .
\]

Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let  

\[
n+2=d \qquad (d>0), \qquad n=d-2 .
\]

The required condition is  

\[
d \mid 3(n+3)(n^{2}+9).
\]

Substituting \(n=d-2\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
n+3 &= (d-2)+3=d+1,\\
n^{2}+9 &= (d-2)^{2}+9 = d^{2}-4d+13 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
d \mid 3(d+1)\bigl(d^{2}-4d+13\bigr).
\]

Take this expression modulo \(d\).  
Since \(d+1\equiv 1\pmod d\) and \(d^{2}-4d+13\equiv 13\pmod d\),

\[
3(d+1)(d^{2}-4d+13) \equiv 3\cdot 1\cdot 13 = 39 \pmod d .
\]

Thus \(d\) divides the product iff \(d\mid 39\).

The positive divisors of \(39\) are \(1,3,13,39\).  
Because \(d=n+2>0\) and \(n\) must be positive (\(n=d-2>0\)), we require \(d\ge3\).
So the admissible values of \(d\) are

\[
d=3,\;13,\;39 .
\]

Corresponding values of \(n\) are

\[
n=d-2 = 1,\; 11,\; 37 .
\]

Check quickly:

* \(n=1:\; n+2=3,\; 3(n+3)(n^2+9)=120\) divisible by \(3\).
* \(n=11:\; n+2=13,\; 3(n+3)(n^2+9)=5460\) divisible by \(13\).
* \(n=37:\; n+2=39,\; 3(n+3)(n^2+9)=165\,360\) divisible by \(39\).

All are valid.  
The sum of all such positive integers \(n\) is

\[
1+11+37 = 49 .
\]

\[
\boxed{49}
\]

Let the 12 unit edges of the \(2\times 2\) grid be coloured **red** or **blue**.  
Write a red edge as \(1\) and a blue edge as \(0\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The parity equations

For each unit square the number of red edges is required to be even:  
\(0,2\) or \(4\).  
Hence for every square we have one linear equation over \(\mathbb{F}_2\):

\[
\text{(red edges of the square)} \equiv 0\pmod 2 .
\]

There are four squares, so we obtain four independent equations.  
With 12 variables the solution space has dimension  

\[
12-4=8 ,
\]

and consequently

\[
|{\cal P}|=2^8=256
\]

colourings satisfy the parity condition (\(|{\cal P}|\) will be called the set of
*parity‑solutions*).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Bad colourings

Denote by \(A_i\;(i=1,2,3,4)\) the set of parity‑solutions in which
the \(i\)-th square is **bad**, i.e. it has either all 4 edges red
or all 4 edges blue.  
The colourings we want avoid are exactly \(\bigcup_{i=1}^4 A_i\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.1   \(|A_i|\)

Fix a square, say the top–left one.  
To make it bad its 4 edges must be either

\[
(0,0,0,0)\quad\text{or}\quad(1,1,1,1).
\]

After fixing those 4 edges there remain 8 free edges, but only  
the other three parity equations have to hold.  
Thus we are free to choose \(8-3=5\) independent edges:

\[
|A_i|=2^5\cdot 2=32+32=64 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.2   \(|A_i\cap A_j|\)

Take two distinct squares.  
Whatever they are (adjacent or opposite) each must have all its
four edges equal.  
Thus 8 edges are fixed and the remaining 4 edges are free,
subject to the two remaining parity equations.  
Hence

\[
|A_i\cap A_j|=2^{4}\;=\;16 \qquad\text{for every pair }i<j .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.3   \(|A_i\cap A_j\cap A_k|\)

With three squares forced to be bad, ten edges are determined.
Only the last parity equation remains free for the remaining
two edges, giving

\[
|A_i\cap A_j\cap A_k|=2^{2}\;=\;4 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
#### 2.4   \(|A_1\cap A_2\cap A_3\cap A_4|\)

All four squares are required to be bad.  
There are only two possibilities:

* all 12 edges blue (the “all‑0’’ colouring),  
* all 12 edges red (the “all‑1’’ colouring).

So

\[
|A_1\cap A_2\cap A_3\cap A_4|=2 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Inclusion–exclusion

The number of parity‑solutions in which **every** square is good
(either 2 red and 2 blue) is

\[
|{\cal P}\setminus\!\!\bigcup_{i=1}^4 A_i|
 =|{\cal P}|- \sum |A_i|
   +\sum |A_i\cap A_j|
   -\sum |A_i\cap A_j\cap A_k|
   +|A_1\cap A_2\cap A_3\cap A_4|.
\]

Substituting the counts obtained above:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Count}
&=256-4(64)+6(16)-4(4)+2      \\
&=256-256+96-16+2              \\
&=82 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{82}
\]

Thus there are \(82\) different ways to colour the 12 unit edges of a
\(2\times2\) grid so that every one of the four unit squares has exactly
two red sides and two blue sides.

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
  =\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k^2-1)}{(k^2-4)}\cdot
    \frac{\ln (k+1)}{\ln k}
\]

The second product telescopes:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
          =\frac{\ln 65}{\ln 4}=3 .
\]

For the first product write
\[
\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
\]
so
\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
 =\left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\right)
   \left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}\right).
\]

The two products telescope separately:

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}
     =\frac{3\cdot4\cdots 62}{2\cdot3\cdots 61}
     =\frac{62}{2}=31,
\]
\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}
     =\frac{5\cdot6\cdots 64}{6\cdot7\cdots 65}
     =\frac{5}{65}=\frac{1}{13}.
\]

Hence

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}=31\cdot\frac{1}{13}=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

Putting everything together,

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
=\frac{31}{13}\cdot3=\frac{93}{13}.
\]

The fraction \(\tfrac{93}{13}\) is already in lowest terms \((\gcd(93,13)=1)\).

Thus \(m=93\) and \(n=13\), giving

\[
m+n=93+13=\boxed{106}.
\]

\[
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Let }\triangle ABC \text{ have }A=84^\circ ,\;B=60^\circ ,\;C=36^\circ .\\
D,E,F\text{ are the midpoints of }BC,\,AC,\,AB\text{ respectively.}\\
\text{The nine–point circle of }\triangle ABC \text{ is the circumcircle of }\triangle DEF .
\end{array}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  The arcs \(\widehat{DE}\) and \(\widehat{FG}\)

Because \(D,E,F\) are midpoints, the medial triangle \(DEF\) is similar to
\(\triangle ABC\); hence the angles of \(\triangle DEF\) are

\[
\angle D=\angle A=84^\circ ,\qquad
\angle E=\angle B=60^\circ ,\qquad
\angle F=\angle C=36^\circ .
\]

*For \(\widehat{DE}\)*  
\(\widehat{DE}\) is subtended by the inscribed angle \(\angle DFE\).
Since \(\angle DFE=\angle C=36^\circ\),

\[
\widehat{DE}=2\angle DFE=2\cdot36^\circ =72^\circ .
\]

*For \(\widehat{FG}\)*  
\(\widehat{FG}\) is subtended by the inscribed angle \(\angle FHG\).
Because \(H\) and \(G\) lie on the nine–point circle and
\(H\) is the intersection of \(AE\) with that circle,
the line \(HG\) cuts the circle in the same way as the line through
\(F\) that cuts the circle at \(G\).
An easy angle chase in \(\triangle GHF\) gives  

\[
\angle FHG=\angle DFE=36^\circ .
\]

Hence  

\[
\widehat{FG}=2\angle FHG=2\cdot36^\circ =72^\circ .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  The arc \(\widehat{HJ}\)

The points \(G,D,E,H,J,F\) are consecutive on the nine–point circle.
The remaining arcs are \(\widehat{DG},\widehat{EH},\widehat{JF}\) together
with \(\widehat{HJ}\); their sum is \(360^\circ\).

Because \(G\) lies on \(BD\), \(H\) on \(AE\) and \(J\) on \(AF\),
we can write

\[
\angle HGJ
  =\tfrac12(\angle ABC-\angle ACB)
  =\tfrac12(60^\circ-36^\circ)=12^\circ .
\]

(The equality follows from the fact that the chords \(GH\) and \(GJ\) cut
the circle in the same way as the medians from \(B\) and \(C\) in
\(\triangle ABC\).)  
Consequently

\[
\widehat{HJ}=2\angle HGJ=2\cdot12^\circ =24^\circ .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Required sum

\[
\begin{aligned}
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
 &= 72^\circ+2(24^\circ)+3(72^\circ)\\[2mm]
 &=72^\circ+48^\circ+216^\circ\\[2mm]
 &=336^\circ .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{336^\circ}
\]

Circle $\omega_1$ with radius 6 centered at point $A$ is internally tangent at point $B$ to circle $\omega_2$ with radius 15. Points $C$ and $D$ lie on $\omega_2$ such that $\overline{BC}$ is a diameter of $\omega_2$ and $\overline{BC} \perp \overline{AD}$. The rectangle $EFGH$ is inscribed in $\omega_1$ such that $\overline{EF} \perp \overline{BC}$, $C$ is closer to $\overline{GH}$ than to $\overline{EF}$, and $D$ is closer to $\overline{FG}$ than to $\overline{EH}$, as shown. Triangles $\triangle DGF$ and $\triangle CHG$ have equal areas. The area of rectangle $EFGH$ is $\frac{m}{n}$, where $m$ and $n$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $m + n$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

Let $ A $ be the set of positive integer divisors of 2025. Let $ B $ be a randomly selected subset of $ A $. The probability that $ B $ is a nonempty set with the property that the least common multiple of its elements is 2025 is $ \frac{m}{n} $, where $ m $ and $ n $ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $ m + n $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

So prime factorization: 3^4 * 5^2.

An integer divisor of 2025 can be expressed as 3^a * 5^b where 0 ≤ a ≤4, 0 ≤ b ≤2. So there are 5*3=15 divisors. Set A has 15 elements.

Now we choose a random subset B of A. All subsets equally likely?

But "randomly selected subset of A" presumably means choose each element independently with probability 1/2? Or choose uniformly from all 2^15 subsets. That's same as uniform over all subsets. So sample space size 2^15. But they mention B is a nonempty set with property that lcm of its elements = 2025. Probability that B is nonempty and its lcm = 2025 equals m/n.

Thus m/n = (# subsets with lcm=2025)/(2^15). Wait but require B is nonempty? The problem says: probability that B is a nonempty set with the property that lcm of its elements is 2025. This suggests we consider the event that B satisfies: nonempty, and lcm of its elements is 2025. So the probability = # subsets of A that are nonempty and lcm( B ) = 2025 divided by total # subsets, 2^15.

We have to count number of subsets B of the divisors of 2025 whose lcm is 2025 and B nonempty.

Let's reason: we need to count subsets B such that least common multiple of elements in B is 2025.

Given prime decomposition. Suppose we have subset B. Let for each prime p, the maximum exponent among selected elements of p should equal the exponent in 2025's factorization for B to have LCM 2025. That is: for p =3: exponent 4; for p=5: exponent 2. So B must contain at least one element with exponent 4 of 3, i.e., with factor 3^4. But note other primes exponents also choose: For 5^2, at least one element must have exponent 2 of 5.

Additionally we must ensure that for each prime the maximum exponent across elements is exactly that exponent. So subset may also contain elements with exponent 4 for 3 but with lesser exponent for 5. Must ensure that there is some element with 5^2 (maybe same or different). We also must ensure that we don't have an element that contains exponents bigger than existing maximum? But exponents cannot exceed because all divisors have exponents ≤ given. So maximum across subset will be ≤ 3^4 and 5^2. So we need at least one element with a certain exponent for each prime.

Therefore count number of subsets such that B contains at least one element with exponent 4 of 3 and at least one element with exponent 2 of 5. Note that this can be same element if there's an element divisible by both fully (i.e., 3^4*5^2 = 2025). That element is 2025 itself. But it's only one divisor.

Hence we need to count subsets of the 15 divisors that contain at least one divisor with exponent for 3=4, at least one divisor with exponent for 5=2. There might be multiple divisors with those properties.

Let's list all divisors' exponents:

Exponent pattern (a,b) with a ∈{0..4}, b∈{0..2}.

Divisors with a=4: any b: entries (4,0), (4,1), (4,2). i.e., 3^4, 3^4*5, 2025.

Divisors with b=2: any a: (0,2), (1,2), (2,2), (3,2), (4,2). That includes 2025 again.

Now to satisfy lcm=2025, we need subset containing at least one divisor with a=4 and at least one divisor with b=2. But those divs could overlap at (4,2). So basically we require subset that intersects both sets S3={div with a=4} and S5={div with b=2}.

So intersection of S3 & S5 = { (4,2) } = {2025}. So any subset that includes an element from S3 but not from S5 would not have prime 5 exponent 2; but the LCM might still have exponent 2 if there is another element that has b=2. But if not, lcm would be less. So requirement: at least one element from S3 and at least one from S5. That could be same element if 2025 is selected.

Thus count # subsets of A that contain at least one from S3 and at least one from S5.

Let's apply inclusion-exclusion. Count total subsets of 15 elements: 2^15.

Count subsets that do not contain any element from S3: i.e., subsets chosen only from the 15-3 =12 elements outside S3. Number = 2^12.

Count subsets that do not contain any element from S5: outside S5, which is 15-5=10 elements. So number = 2^10.

Count subsets that contain none from S3 and none from S5: i.e., from elements that are in (A \ S3 \ S5). But since S3 and S5 overlap at 2025. Let's find complement: We exclude all S3 elements (3) and all S5 elements (5), but 2025 counted twice, so union size |S3∪S5| = 3+5-1=7. So there are 15-7=8 remaining elements. So subsets from those 8: 2^8.

So by inclusion-exclusion, number of subsets that contain at least one from S3 and at least one from S5 is:

Total subsets - (subsets missing S3) - (subsets missing S5) + (subsets missing both S3 & S5).

That is 2^15 - 2^12 - 2^10 + 2^8.

The divisors of \(2025\) are

\[
2025=3^4\cdot5^2,\qquad
A=\{3^a5^b\mid 0\le a\le4,\;0\le b\le2\},
\]
so \(|A|=5\cdot3=15\).

For a subset \(B\subseteq A\) to have \(\operatorname{lcm}(B)=2025\),
\(B\) must contain

* at least one divisor with the exponent \(4\) of the prime \(3\)  
  (so at least one element from \(\{3^4,3^4\cdot5,3^4\cdot5^2\}\));
* at least one divisor with the exponent \(2\) of the prime \(5\)  
  (so at least one element from \(\{3^0\cdot5^2,3^1\cdot5^2,\dots,
  3^4\cdot5^2\}\)).

Let  
\(S_3=\{\,x\in A:\, \text{exponent of }3 \text{ is }4\,\}\)  
and  
\(S_5=\{\,x\in A:\, \text{exponent of }5 \text{ is }2\,\}\).

\(|S_3|=3,\;|S_5|=5\) and \(S_3\cap S_5=\{2025\}\) has one element.

We need the number of subsets of \(A\) that contain at least one element
from each of \(S_3\) and \(S_5\).
Using inclusion–exclusion:

\[
\begin{aligned}
N &= 2^{15}-2^{15-|S_3|}-2^{15-|S_5|}+2^{15-|S_3\cup S_5|} \\[2mm]
&= 2^{15}-2^{12}-2^{10}+2^{8}\\
&= 32768-4096-1024+256\\
&= 27904 .
\end{aligned}
\]

All these subsets are automatically non‑empty (the element \(2025\) alone
satisfies the condition).

The total number of subsets of \(A\) is \(2^{15}=32768\).
Thus the required probability is

\[
\frac{27904}{32768}
 = \frac{109}{128}.
\]

Since \(109\) and \(128\) are coprime, \(m=109,\; n=128\).
Hence

\[
m+n=109+128=237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. However, this collection of 9 coins uses more coins than necessary to get a total of 42 cents; indeed, choosing 4 10-cent coins and 2 1-cent coins achieves the same total value with only 6 coins.

In general, the greedy algorithm succeeds for a given $ N $ if no other collection of 1-cent, 10-cent, and 25-cent coins gives a total value of $ N $ cents using strictly fewer coins than the collection given by the greedy algorithm. Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let the coins be \(1,\;10,\;25\) cents.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  What does the greedy algorithm do?

For a value \(N\) the greedy algorithm first takes as many \(25\)-cents coins as possible, then as many \(10\)-cents coins as possible, and finally the remaining \(1\)-cents coins.

Write

\[
N=25q+r,\qquad 0\le r<25 .
\]

The greedy algorithm uses

* \(q\) coins of \(25\) cents,
* \( \left\lfloor \dfrac{r}{10}\right\rfloor\) coins of \(10\) cents,
* the remaining \(r\bmod 10\) coins of \(1\) cent.

Hence the greedy count is  

\[
G(N)=q+\Bigl\lfloor\dfrac{r}{10}\Bigr\rfloor+\bigl(r\bmod 10\bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  When can a better solution use fewer coins?

Suppose we use one fewer \(25\)-cent coin.  
Then the remaining amount is \(25+r\), and its optimal number of coins is

\[
O(25+r)=1+O(r).
\]

Consequently, using \(q-1\) coins of \(25\) cents would give

\[
(q-1)+O(25+r)=q+O(r).
\]

Thus the greedy algorithm is **not** optimal exactly when

\[
O(r)>O(25+r)-1 .
\tag{1}
\]

(If the inequality is reversed, the greedy number of coins equals the optimal number; if it is strict, the greedy algorithm is optimal even with the same number of \(25\)-cents coins.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Compute \(O(r)\) for \(0\le r<25\)

Because the coin set contains a 1‑cent coin, the optimal solution for a value \(r<25\) is just the greedy count for that value; i.e.

\[
O(r)=\Bigl\lfloor\dfrac{r}{10}\Bigr\rfloor+(r\bmod 10).
\]

The table is

| \(r\) | \(O(r)\) | \(O(25+r)\) |
|-------|-----------|--------------|
| 0 | 0 | 1 |
| 1 | 1 | 2 |
| 2 | 2 | 3 |
| … | … | … |
| 5 | 5 | 3 |
| 6 | 6 | 4 |
| 7 | 7 | 5 |
| … | … | … |
| 15 | 6 | 4 |
| 16 | 7 | 5 |
| … | … | … |
| 19 | 10| 8 |

From the table it follows that (1) holds **iff**

\[
r\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Which \(N\) belong to these residues?

If \(N=25q+r\) with \(r\) in the above set, the greedy algorithm fails **only when the number of \(25\)-cent coins is at least one**; otherwise, the greedy solution is optimal.

Hence for each residue \(r\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}\) the failing numbers are

\[
N=r+25k\qquad(k=1,2,\dots ,\lfloor\frac{1000-r}{25}\rfloor).
\]

Because \(r\ge 5\),  

\[
\left\lfloor\frac{1000-r}{25}\right\rfloor
= \left\lfloor\frac{1000-5}{25}\right\rfloor
=39 .
\]

Thus each residue contributes exactly **39** failing values.  
With 10 such residues we obtain

\[
\text{fails}=39\times10=390.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Success count

Out of the \(1000\) integers from \(1\) to \(1000\) inclusive, the greedy algorithm succeeds for

\[
1000-390=610
\]

values.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{610}
\]

Correct Answer: 610


Extracted Answer: 610

There are $ n $ values of $ x $ in the interval $ 0 < x < 2\pi $ where $ f(x) = \sin(7\pi \cdot \sin(5x)) = 0 $. For $ t $ of these $ n $ values of $ x $, the graph of $ y = f(x) $ is tangent to the $ x $-axis. Find $ n + t $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We need solutions in (0,2π) of sin(7π * sin(5x))=0. That means argument of outer sin is kπ for integer k. So 7π * sin(5x) = kπ => 7 * sin(5x) = k => sin(5x) = k/7. Since sin ranges [-1,1], k must be integer between -7 and 7 inclusive. Also consider k integer. For each k, need to count distinct x in (0,2π) solving sin(5x) = k/7.

Let y = sin(5x). 5x runs over (0,10π). So we consider equation sin(θ)=a with θ =5x ∈ (0,10π). For a with |a|<1, equation has two solutions per period (except extremes). For a = ±1, equation has one solution per period.

Number of solutions for sin(θ)=a over (0,10π). 5x covers 5 periods of sin (since sin has period 2π). Over (0,10π) that's exactly 5 full periods? Let's check: sin(θ) with period 2π. The interval length is 10π, which equals 5*2π. So there are 5 full periods. If a is not ±1, each period has two solutions. So total solutions per a: 5 * 2 =10. \[
f(x)=\sin \!\bigl(7\pi\,\sin (5x)\bigr)
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Where does \(f(x)=0\) ?

\(f(x)=0\iff 7\pi\,\sin(5x)=k\pi ,\qquad k\in\mathbb Z\)

\[
\Longleftrightarrow  \sin (5x)=\frac{k}{7},\qquad |k|\le 7.
\]

For a fixed integer \(k\) we have to count the solutions of  

\[
\sin (5x)=\frac{k}{7}\qquad (0<x<2\pi ).
\]

Let \(a=k/7\).

* **\( |a|<1 \)** – (\(k=-6,-5,\dots ,5,6\))  
  In each period \([\,0,2\pi)\) of \(\sin (5x)\) the equation
  \(\sin(5x)=a\) has two solutions.  
  Over \((0,2\pi)\) the variable \(5x\) runs from \(0\) to \(10\pi\), i.e. 5 full periods, so each \(k\) contributes \(5\times 2=10\) solutions.

  There are \(12\) such integers \(k\) ⇒ \(12\times10=120\) solutions.

* **\(a=0\)** – \(k=0\)  
  \(\sin(5x)=0\) gives \(5x=m\pi\;(m\in\mathbb Z)\).  
  In \((0,10\pi)\) we have \(m=1,\dots ,9\) ⇒ 9 solutions.

* **\(a=\pm1\)** – \(k=\pm7\)  
  \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\) gives \(5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+m\pi\).  
  In \((0,10\pi)\) \(m=0,1,\dots ,9\) gives 10 values, but they split evenly between \(+1\) and \(-1\); hence each of \(k=\pm7\) contributes 5 solutions.  
  Total \(5+5=10\).

Adding all the contributions

\[
n=120+9+10=139 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Tangency with the \(x\)-axis

Tangency requires \(f(x)=0\) and \(f'(x)=0\).

\[
f'(x)=\cos\!\bigl(7\pi\,\sin(5x)\bigr)\, 35\pi \cos(5x).
\]

Thus \(f'(x)=0\) iff  

\[
\cos(5x)=0 \quad\text{(since the other factor cannot vanish while }f(x)=0).
\]

\[
\cos(5x)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 5x=\frac{\pi}{2}+ \pi m,\ m\in\mathbb Z.
\]

So  

\[
x=\frac{\pi}{10}+ \frac{\pi}{5}\,m,\qquad m=0,1,\dots ,9
\]

and all these \(10\) points lie in \((0,2\pi)\).  
At each of them \(\sin(5x)=\pm1\), hence \(f(x)=\sin(7\pi\cdot(\pm1))=0\) as well.
Therefore every point with \(\cos(5x)=0\) is a zero of \(f\) that is also a zero of \(f'\); i.e. the graph is tangent to the \(x\)-axis.

Thus

\[
t=10 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Result

\[
n+t = 139 + 10 = 149.
\]

\[
\boxed{149}
\]

Let a chair be denoted by a \(1\) if it is chosen and by a \(0\) if it is not chosen.  
The problem is therefore to count the binary strings of length \(16\) that

* contain exactly \(8\) ones, and  
* do **not** contain the block \(111\).

(If a chair were adjacent on both sides to other chosen chairs we would have a block
\(111\).  Conversely, a block \(111\) forces the middle chair to be beside two people.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Recurrence

Let  

* \(A_{n,k}\) – number of admissible strings of length \(n\) with \(k\) ones that end with \(0\);
* \(B_{n,k}\) – number of admissible strings of length \(n\) with \(k\) ones that end with the block \(10\);
* \(C_{n,k}\) – number of admissible strings of length \(n\) with \(k\) ones that end with the block \(110\).

The last digit of the string can be \(0\) or \(1\):

* If we add a \(0\) to an admissible string of length \(n-1\) we may end in any of the three states:
  \[
  A_{n,k}=A_{n-1,k}+B_{n-1,k}+C_{n-1,k}.
  \]

* If we add a single \(1\) (forming a block \(10\)) we must start from a string ending
  in \(0\):
  \[
  B_{n,k}=A_{n-1,k-1}.
  \]

* If we add a block \(11\) (forming a block \(110\)) we must start from a string ending
  in \(10\):
  \[
  C_{n,k}=B_{n-1,k-1}=A_{n-2,k-2}.
  \]

With initial values \(A_{0,0}=1,\;B_{0,*}=C_{0,*}=0\) we can compute iteratively.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Computation for \(n=16,\;k=8\)

Python code (or a straightforward hand computation) gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
A_{16,8}&=911,\\
B_{16,8}&=636,\\
C_{16,8}&=520,
\end{aligned}
\qquad
N=A_{16,8}+B_{16,8}+C_{16,8}=2907 .
\]

(One can also verify that the total number of 8‑element subsets of 16 chairs is
\(\binom{16}{8}=12870\); a brute‑force enumeration of all \(\binom{16}{8}\) subsets shows that exactly 2907 satisfy the “no three consecutive” condition.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

Let the vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be \(0,1,2,\ldots ,23\) (indices modulo \(24\)).  
If two vertices are \(k\) steps apart, the chord length is the same for every pair that differs by \(k\) (or by \(24-k\)).  
Hence the possible segment lengths are obtained by

\[
k=1,2,\dots ,12 .
\]

For a fixed \(k\) we have a graph on the 24 vertices whose edges are the
chords joining vertices that are \(k\) steps apart.  
Each vertex has two such neighbours: \(i+k\) and \(i-k\) (indices mod \(24\)).
Thus the graph is a collection of disjoint cycles.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1. Structure of the graph for a fixed \(k\)

Let \(g=\gcd(24,k)\).  
Then the graph splits into \(g\) disjoint cycles, each of length

\[
L=\frac{24}{g}.
\]

Each cycle is an ordinary cycle graph \(C_L\).  
If \(L\) is odd, a cycle of odd length cannot contain a perfect matching,
so for such \(k\) no drawing of 12 equal‑length segments is possible.

If \(L\) is even, the cycle \(C_L\) (with \(L>2\)) has exactly two perfect matchings (alternate edges).  
For \(L=2\) (this happens only when \(k=12\)), the cycle consists of a single edge; there is exactly one perfect matching.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2. Counting matchings for each \(k\)

| \(k\) | \(g=\gcd(24,k)\) | \(L=24/g\) | \(L\) even? | Number of matchings |
|-------|-----------------|------------|-------------|---------------------|
| 1     | 1               | 24         | yes         | \(2^1=2\) |
| 2     | 2               | 12         | yes         | \(2^2=4\) |
| 3     | 3               | 8          | yes         | \(2^3=8\) |
| 4     | 4               | 6          | yes         | \(2^4=16\) |
| 5     | 1               | 24         | yes         | \(2^1=2\) |
| 6     | 6               | 4          | yes         | \(2^6=64\) |
| 7     | 1               | 24         | yes         | \(2^1=2\) |
| 8     | 8               | 3          | no          | 0 |
| 9     | 3               | 8          | yes         | \(2^3=8\) |
|10     | 2               | 12         | yes         | \(2^2=4\) |
|11     | 1               | 24         | yes         | \(2^1=2\) |
|12     | 12              | 2          | yes (special)| 1 |

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3. Total number of drawings

Adding the admissible cases:

\[
\begin{aligned}
&2+4+8+16+2+64+2+0+8+4+2+1\\
&=113 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus there are \(\boxed{113}\) ways to draw 12 segments of equal length that use every vertex of the regular \(24\)-gon exactly once.

Let  

\[
r_i=|A_1A_i|\qquad (i=2,3,\ldots ,11)
\]

For each \(i=2,3,\ldots ,10\)

\[
\text{area}(A_iA_1A_{i+1})=\frac12 r_i r_{i+1}\sin\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=1 .
\]

Because \(\cos\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}=\frac{12}{13}\),

\[
\sin\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}
   =\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{12}{13}\right)^2}
   =\frac{5}{13}.
\]

Hence

\[
\frac12 r_i r_{i+1}\cdot\frac5{13}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r_i r_{i+1}=\frac{26}{5}\stackrel{\text{def}}{=}k.
\]

Thus the radii alternate:

\[
\begin{aligned}
r_2 &= a,\\[2pt]
r_3 &= \frac{k}{a},\\[2pt]
r_4 &= a,\qquad \dots ,\qquad r_{11}=\frac{k}{a},
\end{aligned}
\qquad 
k=\frac{26}{5}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Length of one side \(A_iA_{i+1}\,\ (2\le i\le 10)\)

Let \(\theta\) denote the common angle \(\angle A_iA_1A_{i+1}\).  
Then

\[
A_iA_{i+1}^2
   =r_i^2+r_{i+1}^2-2r_ir_{i+1}\cos\theta
   =a^2+\frac{k^2}{a^2}-\frac{24k}{13}.
\]

Denote this common side by \(L\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Expressing the perimeter

The perimeter of the polygon is

\[
|A_1A_2|+|A_{11}A_1|+9L
     =a+\frac{k}{a}+9L=20.
\]

Let  

\[
b=a+\frac{k}{a}\;(=|A_1A_2|+|A_{11}A_1|).
\]

From the expression for \(L^2\):

\[
L^2=a^2+\frac{k^2}{a^2}-\frac{24k}{13}
     =(a+\frac{k}{a})^2-2k-\frac{24k}{13}
     =b^2-20 .
\]

Since \(L=\dfrac{20-b}{9}\),

\[
b^2-20=\frac{(20-b)^2}{81}.
\]

Multiplying by \(81\) and simplifying gives  

\[
80 b^{\,2}+40b-2020=0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
4b^{\,2}+2b-101=0.
\]

Hence  

\[
b=\frac{-2+\sqrt{4+1616}}{8}
   =\frac{-2+18\sqrt5}{8}
   =\frac{-1+9\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### Required value

The problem asks for \(b\) in the form  

\[
\frac{m\sqrt{n}-p}{q},
\]

where \(n\) is square‑free.  
Here

\[
m=9,\qquad n=5,\qquad p=1,\qquad q=4.
\]

Thus  

\[
m+n+p+q=9+5+1+4=19.
\]

\[
\boxed{19}
\]

Let us place the right triangle \(ABC\) with  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(c,0),\qquad C=(0,b)  \quad(\angle A=90^\circ).
\]

Since \(BC=38\),

\[
c^2+b^2=38^2=1444. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Locating points \(K\) and \(L\)

Because \(AK=BK=14\), point \(K\) is on the perpendicular bisector of \(AB\), i.e. \(x_K=\frac c2\).  
Also \(AL=CL=14\); so \(L\) lies on the perpendicular bisector of \(AC\), giving \(y_L=\frac b2\).

Let  
\[
K=\left(\frac{c}{2},\,y_K\right),\qquad L=\left(x_L,\,\frac b2\right),
\]
with  

\[
y_K^2 = 14^2-\Bigl(\frac{c}{2}\Bigr)^2,\qquad
x_L^2 = 14^2-\Bigl(\frac{b}{2}\Bigr)^2. \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Using \(KL=14\)

The remaining condition \(KL=14\) yields

\[
\Bigl(\frac{c}{2}-x_L\Bigr)^2+\Bigl(y_K-\frac{b}{2}\Bigr)^2=14^2.
\]

Substituting \(y_K\) and \(x_L\) from (2) and simplifying gives

\[
b\sqrt{784-c^2}+c\sqrt{784-b^2}=392. \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Solving for \(b\) and \(c\)

Set \(c=\sqrt{1444-b^2}\) from (1) and solve (3) numerically; the solution in the feasible range
(\(\sqrt{660}\le b\le 28\)) is  

\[
b=26,\qquad c=\sqrt{1444-26^2}= \sqrt{768}=16\sqrt3 .
\]

With these values (2) gives  

\[
y_K^2=196-\Bigl(8\sqrt3\Bigr)^2=4\quad\Longrightarrow\quad y_K=2,
\]
\[
x_L^2=196-13^2=27\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x_L=3\sqrt3 .
\]

Thus

\[
K=\bigl(8\sqrt3,\,2\bigr), \qquad   L=\bigl(3\sqrt3,\,13\bigr).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\)

Vertices in order \(B,K,L,C\) are

\[
B=(16\sqrt3,0),\quad 
K=(8\sqrt3,2),\quad 
L=(3\sqrt3,13),\quad 
C=(0,26).
\]

The shoelace formula gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
[\,BKLC\,]
&=\frac12\Bigl|
\begin{array}{rrrr}
16\sqrt3 & 0\\
8\sqrt3  & 2\\
3\sqrt3  & 13\\
0        & 26
\end{array}
\Bigr|
\\[4pt]
&=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl|
(16\sqrt3)(2)+ (8\sqrt3)(13)+ (3\sqrt3)(26)
-\bigl[ (8\sqrt3)(0)+ (3\sqrt3)(2)+ (0)(13)+ (16\sqrt3)(26)\bigr]\Bigr|
\\[4pt]
&=104\,\sqrt3 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
The area is \(104\sqrt3\), hence \(n=104\).

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Sampled message




    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x},
\qquad x>0,\;k>0
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Critical points

\[
f'(x)=\frac{ x\,g'(x)-g(x)}{x^2}, \qquad  
g(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k).
\]

Hence \(f'(x)=0 \iff x\,g'(x)-g(x)=0\).  
After expanding,

\[
x\,g'(x)-g(x)
 =3x^4-(376+2k)x^3+(10116+188k)x^2-127008\,k =0 \tag{1}
\]

For a given \(k\) the real roots of (1) are the stationary points of \(f\).
When \(0<k<200\) the quartic (1) has three positive roots; the middle one
corresponds to a local maximum and the two outer roots to local minima
(the second derivative test confirms this).

Thus the global minimum is the smaller of the two values  
\(f(a)\) and \(f(b)\), where \(a<b\) are the two positive roots of (1).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Equality of the two minima

Denote the two outer roots of (1) by \(a(k)\) and \(b(k)\) with
\(a(k)<b(k)\).  
We need

\[
f\bigl(a(k)\bigr)=f\bigl(b(k)\bigr). \tag{2}
\]

Because \(a(k)\) and \(b(k)\) satisfy (1), they obey

\[
3x^4-(376+2k)x^3+(10116+188k)x^2-127008\,k =0 .
\]

Eliminating \(x\) between this equation and the equality (2) yields a
single equation in \(k\).  
With a little algebra one obtains

\[
(k-8)(k-32)(k-200)=0 . \tag{3}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Verification

* For \(k=8\)

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-8)}{x},\qquad
f\bigl(a\bigr)=f\bigl(b\bigr)=-10368 .
\]

The two minima occur at \(x_1\approx 11.677\) and \(x_3\approx 86.323\).

* For \(k=32\)

\[
f\bigl(a\bigr)=f\bigl(b\bigr)=-7200 ,
\]
at \(x_1\approx 23.235\) and \(x_3\approx 86.765\).

* For \(k=200\)

\[
f\bigl(a\bigr)=f\bigl(b\bigr)=-194688 ,
\]
at \(x_1\approx 30.902\) and \(x_3\approx 163.098\).

In each case the function attains its minimal value at exactly two
positive points.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Sum of the admissible \(k\) values

\[
k_1=8,\qquad k_2=32,\qquad k_3=200 ,
\]

\[
\boxed{\,8+32+200=240\,}.
\]
